Therefore, for the RANSAC iteration:

- Select three points randomly from map1 (a, b, c)

- Select three points randomly from map2 (d, e, f)

- Now try all 6 permutations of mapping a→d, b→e, c→f and the other permutations.

For each such permutation, compute the rotation and translation between the three points of map1 and map2 (according to the permutation), then count the number of inlier points that are within a threshold distance when transformed.

The choice of which permutation gives the maximum inliers would define a potential candidate model.

Once the best (or a viable) model is found, we track it and after all iterations, choose the top candidate, and use that to find the correspondences for the inliers.

However, such a approach is computationally heavy, but perhaps manageable for 2000 iterations, given that maps may have say 75000 points each, but processing 75000 points for a given model is manageable.

Alternatively, perhaps there's a way to avoid permuting the triplet?

Alternatively, instead of picking 3 points from each, perhaps pick a pair of points from each map, then solve for rotation/translation using these correspondences. Since two points can define a rotation and translation, but there can be two solutions. 

Let me think. With two points, can we find a rigid transformation between them?

Suppose in map 1 we have two points (a and b), and we want to find a transformation so that after applying rotation R and translation T, they correspond to points in map2 (x and y). 

The equations would be:

R*(a) + T = x 

R*(b) + T = y 

Subtract the first equation from the second, you get R*(b - a) = y -x 

Thus, the vector between the two points in map1 after rotation should equal the vector between the map2's corresponding points.

Therefore, the direction and length of the vector between the two points must match up after rotation. 

So, the rotation R can be determined by the direction of the vectors (x - T - a and y-T -b ), which complicates things. Alternatively, solving for R and T.

Let's rearrange:

For x = R*a + T 

y = R*b + T 

Subtract: (y - x) = R*(b -a) 

So R*(b-a) = (y-x). Let v1 = (b-a), v2 = (y-x). 

Then R is such that R*v1 = v2. 

The possible rotation matrices R that satisfy this condition are determined by the angle between v1 and v2, but only two possible rotations (since rotation matrices for 2D can be clockwise or counter-clockwise, depending on orientation).

Thus, there can be two possible rotation matrices for each pair of vectors. 

Once R is determined (there are two possible rotation matrices), then T can be computed as T = x - R*a. 

Once R and T are found for each rotation possibility (there are two possibilities), we can then see for each possibility how many other points in the dataset are inliers. 

Thus, proceeding:

In a RANSAC step:

- Select two points (a,b) from map1.

- Select two points (x,y) from map2.

- Check if the vectors (b -a) and (y -x) have non-zero length. If one has zero length (unlikely), discard.

- Compute the rotation(s) that would map (b-a) to y-x.

Assuming both vectors are non-zero length.

Compute the unit vectors of v1 = (b-a) normalized, v2 = (y-x) normalized.

The angle between them is theta. So the rotation matrix could have an angle of theta or -theta.

Wait, the rotation matrix R could be the one that rotates v1 to v2. Let's think in terms:

Suppose the rotation R is such that R * v1 = v2. 

Then R is determined by the rotation from v1 to v2. Since it's rotation, the direction is important.

Alternatively, perhaps split into the two possibilities where the angle is computed as the angle between v1 and v2, or the opposite direction. 

Once you compute the rotation matrix R for each possible rotation angle, then you compute T, and then check how many points from map1 when transformed align to map2 points.

Therefore, for each pair of points selected in this way, it can generate up to two possible hypothetical transformations. For each of those, the rest of the points can be projected and have their correspondence checked.

If the number of inliers for a particular transformation is sufficiently high, that might represent the true rigid transformation.

Since the problem allows for ~90% correct, this might be achieved by the best transformation among those trials.

The process would be:

- Run many RANSAC trials (each is a random pair from map1 and a random pair from map2)

- For each RANSAC trial, generate the two possible transformations (due to rotation ambiguity)

- For each transformation, count the number of inliers (points from which, when transformed, they have a corresponding point in the other set within a small threshold)

- After all trials, the transformation with the highest inlier count would be selected. 

- Then, using that transformation, find all pairs where when we transform a point from map1, there is a point in map2 within the threshold.

The key here is how to efficiently count inliers for a given transformation.

To do this computationally, perhaps pre-processing: for each point in map2, store their coordinates into a spatial data structure like a k-d tree or grid so that when a point is transformed, a nearest neighbor can be quickly found. 

But doing this during each RANSAC step would be expensive unless it's done once and then queries are fast with the transformation applied.

Alternatively, for each transformation R and T, compute all points from map1 transformed and then, for each such transformed point, check for the closest point in map2 and see if the distance is small enough.

But for a transformation that is a candidate, we can do that for all points in map1 and map2.

However, with N1 and N2 up to 75,000, that's 75,000 x 75,000 for each comparison, which is impossible.

Thus, the way to compute inliers for a given R and T quickly is needed.

Thus, the steps I might need are:

1. Preprocess map2's coordinates into a spatial index, such as a KD-tree, allowing efficient nearest neighbor searches (but I might not be able to do that here due to language restrictions, but assuming we can compute distance for all points quickly enough).

Wait, after applying transformation R and T to a point in map1's set, the transformed point should be close to some point in map2's set.

Thus, for eachtransformed point in map1, we can compute its location, then find the closest point in map2. 

Alternatively, for all points in map2, precompute and have them as a list, and compute distance squared for the transformed point to each map2 point.

But for 75,000 points, this would be O(N2) per query which might be too slow for many RANSAC iterations.

Thus, the key is to find an efficient way to compute for a transformed point (p'), the nearest neighbor in map2's points. 

Possibly, the easiest way for code submission would be to:

- Precompute a sorted list of points in map2 sorted in some coordinate order, say by x, then y, and for each transformed point, find points within a certain distance in a certain range, but even that may not be very efficient but perhaps manageable for small number of iterations when combined with a good distance threshold.

Alternatively, perhaps instead of exact distance, for each map2 point, for each R, T compute the transform of points but that's too time-consuming.

Hmm, this is a problem. Let's think of possible optimizations.

Suppose, the number of RANSAC iterations are manageable. Like 10000 iterations, which can be done in time. Although each iteration may have some computations.

Alternatively, here's an approach:

In each RANSAC iteration that produces a candidate transformation (R and T), the inliers can be estimated by taking a sample of N1 points in map1, transforming them, and seeing how many of them have any map2 points within the threshold distance, expressed as a ratio, allowing for a quick approximation. But this would be an approximation, but maybe sufficient.

Alternatively, here's a different idea inspired by the fact that the noise in the data is only extra points; the inlier points (the Death stars) are the same points in both maps. Thus the real transformation between them would mean that the Death stars' points in map1 when transformed should exactly correspond (within precision errors) to those in map2. So the distance between transformed map1 death star points and map2 points should be very small.

    59	By using this libraries, you'll ensure a robust and unified interface for your<#sep#>import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.utils.data
import torch.utils
from matplotlib import cm as cm
from torch.nn.parallel import *
from torchvision.models import *
from torchvision.datasets import *


class ChannelSELayer(nn.Module):
    """
    Copied from https://github.com/ai-med/squeeze_and_excitation/blob/master/squeeze_and_excitation/squeeze_and_excitation.py

    Re-implementation of Squeeze-and-Excitation (SE) block described in:
        *Hu et al., Squeeze-and-Excitation Networks, arXiv:1709.01507*

    MIT License

    Copyright (c) 2018 Abhijit Guha Roy

    Permission is hereby granted, free of charge, to any person obtaining a copy
    of this software and associated documentation files (the "Software"), to deal
    in the Software without restriction, including without limitation the rights
    to use, copy, modify, merge, publish, distribute, sublicense, and/or sell
    copies of the Software, and to permit persons to whom the Software is
    furnished to do so, subject to the following conditions:

    The above copyright notice and this permission notice shall be included in all
    copies or substantial portions of the Software.

    THE SOFTWARE IS PROVIDED "AS IS", WITHOUT WARRANTY OF ANY KIND, EXPRESS OR
    IMPLIED, INCLUDING BUT NOT LIMITED TO THE WARRANTIES OF MERCHANTABILITY,
    FITNESS FOR A PARTICULAR PURPOSE AND NONINFRINGEMENT. IN NO EVENT SHALL THE
    AUTHORS OR COPYRIGHT HOLDERS BE LIABLE FOR ANY CLAIM, DAMAGES OR OTHER
    LIABILITY, WHETHER IN AN ACTION OF CONTRACT, TORT OR OTHERWISE, ARISING FROM,
    OUT OF OR IN CONNECTION WITH THE SOFTWARE OR THE USE OR OTHER DEALINGS IN THE
    SOFTWARE.

    """

    def __init__(self, num_channels, reduction_ratio=2, dim_out=None, stride=1
        ):
        """
        :param num_channels: No of input channels
        :param reduction_ratio: By how much should the num_channels should be reduced
        """
        super(ChannelSELayer, self).__init__()
        if dim_out is not None:
            assert dim_out == num_channels, (dim_out, num_channels,
                'only same dimensionality is supported')
        num_channels_reduced = num_channels // reduction_ratio
        self.reduction_ratio = reduction_ratio
        self.stride = stride
        self.fc1 = nn.Linear(num_channels, num_channels_reduced, bias=True)
        self.fc2 = nn.Linear(num_channels_reduced, num_channels, bias=True)
        self.relu = nn.ReLU()
        self.sigmoid = nn.Hardswish()

    def forward(self, input_tensor):
        """
        :param input_tensor: X, shape = (batch_size, num_channels, H, W)
        :return: output tensor
        """
        batch_size, num_channels, _H, _W = input_tensor.size()
        squeeze_tensor = input_tensor.reshape(batch_size, num_channels, -1
            ).mean(dim=2)
        fc_out_1 = self.relu(self.fc1(squeeze_tensor))
        fc_out_2 = self.sigmoid(self.fc2(fc_out_1))
        a, b = squeeze_tensor.size()
        output_tensor = torch.mul(input_tensor, fc_out_2.view(a, b, 1, 1))
        if self.stride > 1:
            output_tensor = output_tensor[:, :, ::self.stride, ::self.stride]
        return output_tensor


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'num_channels': 4}]

   - For sums from 10 to 18, the sum of the digits is calculated as follows:
     - Sum 10: \(1 + 0 = 1\)
     - Sum 11: \(1 + 1 = 2\)
     - Sum 12: \(1 + 2 = 3\)
     - Sum 13: \(1 + 3 = 4\)
     - Sum 14: \(1 + 4 = 5\)
     - Sum 15: \(1 + 5 = 6\)
     - Sum 16: \(1 + 6 = 7\)
     - Sum 17: \(1 + 7 = 8\)
     - Sum 18: \(1 + 8 = 9\)

4. **Calculate the contribution of each sum to the expectation:**
   - For each sum \(s\), the contribution to the expectation is the sum of the digits multiplied by the probability of that sum.
   - The contributions are as follows:
     - Sum 3: \(3 \times \frac{1}{216} = \frac{3}{216}\)
     - Sum 4: \(4 \times \frac{3}{216} = \frac{12}{216}\)
     - Sum 5: \(5 \times \frac{6}{216} = \frac{30}{216}\)
     - Sum 6: \(6 \times \frac{10}{216} = \frac{60}{216}\)
     - Sum 7: \(7 \times \frac{15}{216} = \frac{105}{216}\)
     - Sum 8: \(8 \times \frac{21}{216} = \frac{168}{216}\)
     - Sum 9: \(9 \times \frac{25}{216} = \frac{225}{216}\)
     - Sum 10: \(1 \times \frac{27}{216} = \frac{27}{216}\)
     - Sum 11: \(2 \times \frac{27}{216} = \frac{54}{216}\)
     - Sum 12: \(3 \times \frac{25}{216} = \frac{75}{216}\)
     - Sum 13: \(4 \times \frac{21}{216} = \frac{84}{216}\)
     - Sum 14: \(5 \times \frac{15}{216} = \frac{75}{216}\)
     - Sum 15: \(6 \times \frac{10}{216} = \frac{60}{216}\)
     - Sum 16: \(7 \times \frac{6}{216} = \frac{42}{216}\)
     - Sum 17: \(8 \times \frac{3}{216} = \frac{24}{216}\)
     - Sum 18: \(9 \times \frac{1}{216} = \frac{9}{216}\)

5. **Sum the contributions to find the expectation:**
   - The total expectation is the sum of all contributions:
     \[
     \frac{3}{216} + \frac{12}{216} + \frac{30}{216} + \frac{60}{216} + \frac{105}{216} + \frac{168}{216} + \frac{225}{216} + \frac{27}{216} + \frac{54}{216} + \frac{75}{216} + \frac{84}{216} + \frac{75}{216} + \frac{60}{216} + \frac{42}{216} + \frac{24}{216} + \frac{9}{216} = \frac{1053}{216}
     \]

6. **Simplify the fraction:**
   - Simplify \(\frac{1053}{216}\) by dividing the numerator and the denominator by their greatest common divisor, which is 27:
     \[
     \frac{1053 \div 27}{216 \div 27} = \frac{39}{8}
     \]

Therefore, the mathematical expectation of the sum of the digits of the numbers resulting from the sum of three dice is \(\boxed{\frac{39}{8}}\).<#sep#> how many of them have any map2 points within the threshold distance, expressed as a ratio, allowing for a quick approximation. Thus the real transformation between them would mean that the Death stars' points in map1 when transformed should exactly correspond (within precision errors) to those in map2. So the distance between transformed map1 death star points and map2 points should be very small.

So, the process would be:

Once a candidate transformation is built from a trial in RANSAC (like from the two points and one of the two rotations), then:

- for each point in map1 (say, point p), compute its transformed location q = R*p + T.

- Then, look for any point in map2 within say distance 0.1 (due to the two decimal precision).

- The count of such points (the point in map1 corresponds to a point in map2 within that distance) will be the inlier count for this transformation. 

Computing for all N1 map1 points: each point is transformed, then query against map2 coordinates. However doing this for each RANSAC trial is O((N1 + N2)*N1) at worst, which is too much (for N1 ~ 50000, this is quadratic).

Alternative Idea: Using a hash grid.

Suppose that we discretize the coordinates into a grid, so that any two points within distance ~ delta (the threshold distance) will be in the same or adjacent cells. 

For map2's points, we can pre-process them into such a grid. So, for each point in map2, we can note its grid cell coordinates, which can be (round(x/delta), round(y/delta)), for some delta (say 0.1 or something). 

Then, when a transformed point p' is computed, its cell is (floor(p'.x/delta), floor(p'.y/delta)). Then, to see if any map2 points are "close", we can check all map2 points in that cell and the 8 adjacent cells. The points in those cells are the possible candidates for being within a threshold. 

The advantage is that for each transformed p', checking 9 cells can be quick if the cells are stored in a hash map (like a dictionary, where the key is the (x,y) grid coordinates and the value is a list of points in that cell). 

Thus, to compute the inlier count:

1. Precompute the grid for map2 once at the beginning. Choose delta as the threshold, say 0.1 units.

2. For each RANSAC trial's candidate transformation (Rotation rotation, Translation Tx, Ty), transformed points in map1 are calculated as q = R*m.x + Tx, R*m.y + Ty ?

Wait more precisely: The transformation is QRx + T, where R is rotation matrix (2x2), then translate T = (Tx, Ty). 

So, for a point (x,y), the transformed coordinates would be (new_x, new_y) = R*(x,y) + T.

Ah right, so that's a vector addition. 

The idea is:

- For a given candidate transformation (R and T), compute for each of the 3 test points in RANSAC the transformed positions, which will match the chosen points in map2 within the threshold (the reason why the R and T was chosen).

For each trial, to compute inlier count:

- We iterate over all points in map1, compute their transformed coordinates (q_x, q_y), and look in the grid for map2's grid around (qx, qy) with threshold, then if there is any map2 point in any nearby grid cell within threshold.

Count all such points. This way, for each map1 point, the check is O(1) or O(9 * per cell points), but with a manageable constant factor. 

However, with N1 up to 50,000, and for 100,000 iterations (RANSAC steps), that would be 50,000 * 100,000 = 5 billion operations. Which is way too slow.

Hence, this suggests that even with this grid, the computation would be too slow. Therefore, perhaps a better approach is to use a Monte Carlo approach, checking only a random subset of points for inliers in each iteration. This reduces the cost but still gives an approximate inlier count. However, the variance might be high.

- To choose an initial random sample of three points from map1, and three from map2.

But the correspondence between them is the critical part. For each triplet selected between map1 and map2, the correspondence is ambiguous, but perhaps can be permuted until finding the one that gives the best fit.

- For each selected triplet in map1, select all three points in map2 and try different triplets to see which permutation of the selected triplet gives the best transformation.

Alternatively, pick three points from map1, and for the map2 points, compute which three points in map2 can form a rigid transform that maps the three selected map1 points to them. To compute this, we can loop over the map2 points in groups of three and attempt them. However, this is O(N choose 3), which is prohibitive for large N2.

import torch
from torch.nn import functional as F
from torch.nn import MultiheadAttention
from torch.nn import Dropout
from torch.nn import Linear
from torch.nn import LayerNorm


def _get_activation_fn(activation):
    if activation == 'relu':
        return F.relu
    elif activation == 'gelu':
        return F.gelu
    raise RuntimeError('activation should be relu/gelu, not {}'.format(
        activation))


class TransformerDecoderLayer(Module):
    """TransformerDecoderLayer is made up of self-attn, multi-head-attn and feedforward network.
    This standard decoder layer is based on the paper "Attention Is All You Need".
    Ashish Vaswani, Noam Shazeer, Niki Parmar, Jakob Uszkoreit, Llion Jones, Aidan N Gomez,
    Lukasz Kaiser, and Illia Polosukhin. 2017. Attention is all you need. In Advances in
    Neural Information Processing Systems, pages 6000-6010. Users may modify or implement
    in a different way during application.

    Args:
        d_model: the number of expected features in the input (required).
        nhead: the number of heads in the multiheadattention models (required).
        dim_feedforward: the dimension of the feedforward network model (default=2048).
        dropout: the dropout value (default=0.1).
        activation: the activation function of intermediate layer, relu or gelu (default=relu).

    Examples::
        >>> decoder_layer = nn.TransformerDecoderLayer(d_model=512, nhead=8)
        >>> memory = torch.rand(10, 32, 512)
        >>> tgt = torch.rand(20, 32, 512)
        >>> out = decoder_layer(tgt, memory)
    """

    def __init__(self, d_model, nhead, dim_feedforward=2048, dropout=0.1,
        activation='relu'):
        super(TransformerDecoderLayer, self).__init__()
        self.self_attn = MultiheadAttention(d_model, nhead, dropout=dropout)
        self.multihead_attn = MultiheadAttention(d_model, nhead, dropout=
            dropout)
        self.linear1 = Linear(d_model, dim_feedforward)
        self.dropout = Dropout(dropout)
        self.linear2 = Linear(dim_feedforward, d_model)
        self.norm1 = LayerNorm(d_model)
        self.norm2 = LayerNorm(d_model)
        self.norm3 = LayerNorm(d_model)
        self.dropout1 = Dropout(dropout)
        self.dropout2 = Dropout(dropout)
        self.dropout3 = Dropout(dropout)
        self.activation = _get_activation_fn(activation)

    def __setstate__(self, state):
        if 'activation' not in state:
            state['activation'] = F.relu
        super(TransformerDecoderLayer, self).__setstate__(state)

    def forward(self, tgt, memory, tgt_mask=None, memory_mask=None,
        tgt_key_padding_mask=None, memory_key_padding_mask=None):
        """Pass the inputs (and mask) through the decoder layer.

        Args:
            tgt: the sequence to the decoder layer (required).
            memory: the sequence from the last layer of the encoder (required).
            tgt_mask: the mask for the tgt sequence (optional).
            memory_mask: the mask for the memory sequence (optional).
            tgt_key_padding_mask: the mask for the tgt keys per batch (optional).
            memory_key_padding_mask: the mask for the memory keys per batch (optional).

        Shape:
            see the docs in Transformer class.
        """
        tgt2 = self.self_attn(tgt, tgt, tgt, attn_mask=tgt_mask,
            key_padding_mask=tgt_key_padding_mask)[0]
        tgt = tgt + self.dropout1(tgt2)
        tgt = self.norm1(tgt)
        tgt2, attention = self.multihead_attn(tgt, memory, memory,
            attn_mask=memory_mask, key_padding_mask=memory_key_padding_mask)
        tgt = tgt + self.dropout2(tgt2)
        tgt = self.norm2(tgt)
        tgt2 = self.linear2(self.dropout(self.activation(self.linear1(tgt))))
        tgt = tgt + self.dropout3(tgt2)
        tgt = self.norm3(tgt)
        return tgt, attention


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'d_model': 4, 'nhead': 4}]
